**Objective**: Generate a semantically equivalent vectorize-friendly version of `*_opt` using:  
**Preservation Requirements**:  
1. Function signature

If the original value is used, e.g. a[i+1] is used for iteration i, a[i+1] should be stored in a temporary variable(use memcpy to store orign value) or used before updating a[i+1]
If the updated value is used, e.g. a[i-1] is used for iteration i, a[i-1] should update before using it.

**Possible Transformations**  
Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops  

Use temporary variable:
Use temp variable to store result to avoid complex memory access

Remove branching logic (if, else if, switch) from loops to enable automatic vectorization:
Replace multi-way conditionals with arithmetic masking, ensuring uniform execution paths across iterations.
**When to Apply**:  
    - Example:  
     ```c
    // Original
    if (cond1)  
        x = value_A;  
    else if (cond2)  
        x = value_B;  
    else  
        x = value_C;
    // Optimized  
    x = cond1 * value_A + (cond2 && !cond1) * value_B + (!cond1 && !cond2) * value_C;  
     ```

If the access pattern involves using a[i-1] and updating a[i] within the same iteration, a dependency exists. Attempt to reorder the instructions to resolve this dependency.

## **2. Semantic Comparison with Simple Test Cases**

The original loop:

```c
aa[j][i] = (aa[j][i-1] + aa[j-1][i]) / 1.9;
```

- Uses `aa[j][i-1]` from the **current row**, already updated in this iteration of `i`.
- Uses `aa[j-1][i]` from the **previous row**, which is from the previous iteration of `j` (and not updated in this `nl` iteration except in earlier `j`).

The optimized code attempts to use:
- `left_val` for `aa[j][i-1]` (updated as we go across the row).
- `prev_row[i]` for `aa[j-1][i]`.

But there’s a **critical bug** in the optimized version:

Inside the inner loop:
```c
float new_val = (left_val + prev_row[i]) / 1.9f;
prev_row[i] = aa[j][i];   // <-- This overwrites prev_row[i] BEFORE we finish using it for all i in this j?
aa[j][i] = new_val;
left_val = new_val;
```

Actually, `prev_row[i]` is supposed to hold `aa[j-1][i]` for the **current j loop**.  
But here, `prev_row[i] = aa[j][i];` overwrites `prev_row[i]` with the **old** `aa[j][i]` (before update) — but `aa[j][i]` hasn’t been changed yet in this `nl` iteration except by previous `i` iterations? Wait, `aa[j][i]` is the original value from previous `nl` iteration or initial.

But the problem:  
For `i=1`, `prev_row[1]` is `aa[j-1][1]` (correct).  
But after computing `new_val` for `i=1`, we do `prev_row[1] = aa[j][1]` (old value).  
Then for `i=2`, `prev_row[2]` is still `aa[j-1][2]` (unchanged so far), correct.  
But after `i=2`, `prev_row[2] = aa[j][2]` (old value).

So for the next `j+1`, `prev_row[i]` is now `aa[j][i]` **old** (from start of current `j` iteration), not updated values.  
But the original algorithm for `j+1` uses `aa[j][i]` **updated** in the previous `j` iteration.

Thus **semantics are broken**.

---

**Example check** (small 3x3, one nl iteration):

`aa[1][1] = (aa[1][0] + aa[0][1])/1.9`  
`j=1,i=2`:  
`aa[1][2] = (aa[1][1] + aa[0][2])/1.9` — uses **updated** `aa[1][1]`.

Optimized, `j=1,i=1`:  
`new_val = (aa[1][0] + aa[0][1])/1.9`, store to `aa[1][1]`, `prev_row[1] = old aa[1][1]`.  
`j=1,i=2`:  
`new_val = (new_val + aa[0][2])/1.9` — correct so far? Wait, `prev_row[2]` is still `aa[0][2]`, yes.

But after finishing `j=1`, we copy `aa[1][i]` (updated values) to `prev_row`.  
Then `j=2,i=1`:  
`new_val = (aa[2][0] + aa[1][1])/1.9` — `aa[1][1]` is updated value, correct.

So maybe the bug is not in the data flow for `prev_row` after all? Let’s check the final `prev_row` update:  
At the end of each `j`, we do `prev_row[i] = aa[j][i]` (updated values). That’s correct.

But wait — there’s an **extra** `prev_row[i] = aa[j][i]` **inside** the inner loop. That’s wrong because it corrupts `prev_row` for later `i` in the same `j`? No — it only corrupts `prev_row[i]` for current `i`, but `prev_row[i]` for current `i` is not used again in same `j` iteration (since `i` increments). So maybe safe? But it’s unnecessary.

Actually, the real bug:  
Inside inner loop, `prev_row[i] = aa[j][i]` (old value) — but `aa[j][i]` is **old** from before update in this `nl` iteration. That’s fine because `prev_row[i]` for current `i` not used again in same `j`.  
But after inner loop, we copy `aa[j][i]` (updated) to `prev_row[i]` again — overwriting the old stored one. So inner-loop assignment is useless.

But there’s still a subtlety:  
What about `prev_row[i]` for `i` less than current `i`? They were overwritten inside loop earlier in this `j`, so they now hold **old** `aa[j][i]` values, not `aa[j-1][i]`.  
But we don’t use them again for same `j`. So maybe okay.

But wait — in original, `aa[1][0]` is never updated in `j=1` iteration because `i` starts at 1. So `aa[1][0]` is 4. Yes.

---

## **1. Compilation errors in optimized code**

## **2. Semantic comparison**

The original loop:

```c
aa[j][i] = (aa[j][i-1] + aa[j-1][i]) / 1.9;
```

- Uses `aa[j][i-1]` from the **same row, previous column** (updated in current iteration of `i`).
- Uses `aa[j-1][i]` from the **previous row, same column** (updated in previous iteration of `j`).

This is a **wavefront** pattern: each cell depends on left neighbor (current row) and top neighbor (previous row).

---

The optimized code uses:

```c
float left_val = aa[j][0];
for (int i = 1; i < 256; i++) {
    float new_val = (left_val + prev_row[i]) / 1.9f;
    aa[j][i] = new_val;
    left_val = new_val;
}
```

Here:
- `left_val` starts as `aa[j][0]` (original value at column 0 of current row).
- `prev_row[i]` contains `aa[j-1][i]` from before updating row `j`.
- `new_val` is stored to `aa[j][i]`, then `left_val` becomes `new_val` for next `i`.

This matches the original dependencies exactly:  
`aa[j][i-1]` is the just-computed `new_val` from previous `i` (stored in `left_val`),  
`aa[j-1][i]` is `prev_row[i]` (original top neighbor before row `j` updates).

So **semantics are correct**.

---

Let’s test with a small example manually:

Initial `aa` (2x2 for simplicity):

```
[1, 2]
[3, 4]
```

Original code for `j=1, i=1`:
`aa[1][1] = (aa[1][0] + aa[0][1]) / 1.9 = (3 + 2) / 1.9 ≈ 2.63158`.

Optimized code:
`prev_row = [1, 2]`, `left_val = aa[1][0] = 3`.
`i=1`: `new_val = (3 + 2) / 1.9 ≈ 2.63158`. ✅

Matches.

---

## **3. Vectorization analysis**

Compiler feedback says:

> loop not vectorized: value that could not be identified as reduction is used outside the loop

This refers to the inner `i` loop in optimized code:

```c
for (int i = 1; i < 256; i++) {
    float new_val = (left_val + prev_row[i]) / 1.9f;
    aa[j][i] = new_val;
    left_val = new_val;
}
```

The problem: `left_val` is a **loop-carried scalar dependency** (each iteration uses `left_val` from previous iteration, updated with `new_val`).  
This is a **true dependency** (RAW), not a reduction pattern the compiler recognizes, so it cannot vectorize this loop directly.

---

In the **original code**, the dependency on `aa[j][i-1]` is also loop-carried in the `i` loop, so original inner loop is **not vectorizable** either.  
But the optimized code didn’t improve vectorization; it just made the scalar dependency explicit (`left_val`).

---

## **4. Can we make it vectorizable?**

For vectorization, we need to break the loop-carried dependency on `aa[j][i-1]`.  
But this is a fundamental data dependency in the recurrence:  
`aa[j][i] = f(aa[j][i-1], aa[j-1][i])`.

This is a **sequential scan** along each row (after top row is fixed from previous iteration of `j`).  
Such recurrence cannot be parallelized/vectorized in the straightforward way without changing algorithm (e.g., using parallel prefix scan techniques).

Thus, **automatic vectorization is not possible** for the inner loop in either original or optimized version due to true dependency.

---

## **5. Optimization opportunity**

The optimized code already improved spatial locality by using `prev_row` array (better cache reuse for top neighbor values).  
But it didn’t enable vectorization.

We could try **loop tiling** for better cache performance, but the inner loop still can’t be vectorized due to recurrence.

Given the compiler feedback and the nature of the dependency, no further simple modification will make this vectorizable without algorithmic change (which would change semantics).

## **2. Semantic comparison**

The original loop:

```c
aa[j][i] = (aa[j][i-1] + aa[j-1][i]) / 1.9;
```

- Uses `aa[j][i-1]` from the **same row, previous column** (updated in current iteration of `i`).
- Uses `aa[j-1][i]` from the **previous row, same column** (updated in previous iteration of `j`).

This is a **wavefront** pattern: each cell depends on left neighbor (current row) and top neighbor (previous row).

---

The optimized code uses:

```c
float left_val = aa[j][0];
for (int i = 1; i < 256; i++) {
    float new_val = (left_val + prev_row[i]) / 1.9f;
    aa[j][i] = new_val;
    left_val = new_val;
}
```

Here:
- `left_val` starts as `aa[j][0]` (original value at column 0 of current row).
- `prev_row[i]` contains `aa[j-1][i]` from before updating row `j`.
- `new_val` is stored to `aa[j][i]`, then `left_val` becomes `new_val` for next `i`.

This matches the original dependencies exactly:  
`aa[j][i-1]` is the just-computed `new_val` from previous `i` (stored in `left_val`),  
`aa[j-1][i]` is `prev_row[i]` (original top neighbor before row `j` updates).

So **semantics are correct**.

---

Initial `aa` (2x2 for simplicity):

```
[1, 2]
[3, 4]
```

`aa[1][1] = (aa[1][0] + aa[0][1]) / 1.9 = (3 + 2) / 1.9 ≈ 2.63158`.

Optimized code:
`prev_row = [1, 2]`, `left_val = aa[1][0] = 3`.
`i=1`: `new_val = (3 + 2) / 1.9 ≈ 2.63158`. ✅

Matches.

## **3. Vectorization analysis**

Compiler feedback says:

> loop not vectorized: value that could not be identified as reduction is used outside the loop

This refers to the inner `i` loop in optimized code:

```c
for (int i = 1; i < 256; i++) {
    float new_val = (left_val + prev_row[i]) / 1.9f;
    aa[j][i] = new_val;
    left_val = new_val;
}
```

The problem: `left_val` is a **loop-carried scalar dependency** (each iteration uses `left_val` from previous iteration, updated with `new_val`).  
This is a **true dependency** (RAW), not a reduction pattern the compiler recognizes, so it cannot vectorize this loop directly.

---

In the **original code**, the dependency on `aa[j][i-1]` is also loop-carried in the `i` loop, so original inner loop is **not vectorizable** either.  
But the optimized code didn’t improve vectorization; it just made the scalar dependency explicit (`left_val`).

---

## **4. Can we make it vectorizable?**

For vectorization, we need to break the loop-carried dependency on `aa[j][i-1]`.  
But this is a fundamental data dependency in the recurrence:  
`aa[j][i] = f(aa[j][i-1], aa[j-1][i])`.

This is a **sequential scan** along each row (after top row is fixed from previous iteration of `j`).  
Such recurrence cannot be parallelized/vectorized in the straightforward way without changing algorithm (e.g., using parallel prefix scan techniques).

Thus, **automatic vectorization is not possible** for the inner loop in either original or optimized version due to true dependency.

---

## **5. Optimization opportunity**

The optimized code already improved spatial locality by using `prev_row` array (better cache reuse for top neighbor values).  
But it didn’t enable vectorization.

We could try **loop tiling** for better cache performance, but the inner loop still can’t be vectorized due to recurrence.

Given the compiler feedback and the nature of the dependency, no further simple modification will make this vectorizable without algorithmic change (which would change semantics).

---

We could process multiple rows in parallel (wavefront diagonal method) or use SIMD for vertical operations if we reorder loops, but that changes access pattern.  
The current optimized code doesn’t explore that, so there remains opportunity → **FAIL**.

The original loop:

```c
for (int j = 1; j < 256; j++) {

        aa[j][i] = (aa[j][i-1] + aa[j-1][i]) / 1.9;
    }
}
```

This has a **true dependency** on `aa[j][i-1]` (horizontal, within the same row) and on `aa[j-1][i]` (vertical, from previous row).  
The vertical dependency is fine — previous row’s values are from the previous iteration of `j`, already computed.  
The horizontal dependency is **sequential within each row**: `aa[j][i]` depends on `aa[j][i-1]`, which was computed in the same `j` iteration just before.

The optimized code attempts to break this horizontal dependency by using `temp_result[i] = (aa[j][i-1] + prev_row[i]) / 1.9f;`  
But here `aa[j][i-1]` is still the **original** value from before the loop, not the updated value from `temp_result[i-1]`.  
This is wrong — the original algorithm uses the **newly computed** `aa[j][i-1]`, not the old one.

Let’s test with a tiny 3×3 example:

Row 1: 1 0 0  
Row 2: 1 0 0

Then `i=2`:  
`aa[1][2] = (aa[1][1] + aa[0][2])/1.9 = (1.0526 + 1)/1.9 ≈ 1.0803`

**Optimized algorithm** for `j=1`:  
`temp_result[1] = (aa[1][0] + prev_row[1])/1.9 = (1 + 1)/1.9 = 1.0526`  
`temp_result[2] = (aa[1][1] + prev_row[2])/1.9 = (0 + 1)/1.9 ≈ 0.5263` ← wrong!  
Because it used old `aa[1][1] = 0`, not the new 1.0526.

`left_val` is a scalar that carries a dependency across iterations (true loop-carried dependency).  
It’s not a reduction (reduction would be like `sum += a[i]`), it’s a recurrence where each iteration depends on the previous iteration’s result.

So if we precompute `pow(1/1.9, i)` and do a prefix-sum-like transformation, we could vectorize.

**Possible Transformations**  
Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```

   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops

void s2111_opt(int iterations, float aa[256][256]) {
    for (int nl = 0; nl < 100 * (iterations / 256); nl++) {
        float prev_row[256];
        
        // Copy row 0 to prev_row
        for (int i = 0; i < 256; i++) {
            prev_row[i] = aa[0][i];
        }
        
        for (int j = 1; j < 256; j++) {
            // Store original row j before modification
            float current_row[256];
            for (int i = 0; i < 256; i++) {
                current_row[i] = aa[j][i];
            }
            
            // Process row j - separate dependency chains
            float left_val = current_row[0];
            for (int i = 1; i < 256; i++) {
                // Use original values from current_row for horizontal dependency
                // and updated values from prev_row for vertical dependency
                left_val = (left_val + prev_row[i]) / 1.9f;
                aa[j][i] = left_val;
            }
            
            // Update prev_row with newly computed values for next row
            for (int i = 0; i < 256; i++) {
                prev_row[i] = aa[j][i];
            }
        }
    }
}


**Objective**: Generate a semantically equivalent vectorize-friendly version of `*_opt` using:  
**Preservation Requirements**:  
1. Function signature

If the original value is used, e.g. a[i+1] is used for iteration i, a[i+1] should be stored in a temporary variable(use memcpy to store orign value) or used before updating a[i+1]
If the updated value is used, e.g. a[i-1] is used for iteration i, a[i-1] should update before using it.

**Possible Transformations**  
Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops  

Use temporary variable:
Use temp variable to store result to avoid complex memory access

Remove branching logic (if, else if, switch) from loops to enable automatic vectorization:
Replace multi-way conditionals with arithmetic masking, ensuring uniform execution paths across iterations.
**When to Apply**:  
    - Example:  
     ```c
    // Original
    if (cond1)  
        x = value_A;  
    else if (cond2)  
        x = value_B;  
    else  
        x = value_C;
    // Optimized  
    x = cond1 * value_A + (cond2 && !cond1) * value_B + (!cond1 && !cond2) * value_C;  
     ```

If the access pattern involves using a[i-1] and updating a[i] within the same iteration, a dependency exists. Attempt to reorder the instructions to resolve this dependency.

}

**Preservation Requirements**:  
1. Function signature

If the original value is used, e.g. a[i+1] is used for iteration i, a[i+1] should be stored in a temporary variable(use memcpy to store orign value) or used before updating a[i+1]
If the updated value is used, e.g. a[i-1] is used for iteration i, a[i-1] should update before using it.

**Possible Transformations**  
Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops  

Use temporary variable:
Use temp variable to store result to avoid complex memory access

Remove branching logic (if, else if, switch) from loops to enable automatic vectorization:
Replace multi-way conditionals with arithmetic masking, ensuring uniform execution paths across iterations.
**When to Apply**:  
    - Example:  
     ```c
    // Original
    if (cond1)  
        x = value_A;  
    else if (cond2)  
        x = value_B;  
    else  
        x = value_C;
    // Optimized  
    x = cond1 * value_A + (cond2 && !cond1) * value_B + (!cond1 && !cond2) * value_C;  
     ```

**Possible Transformations**  
Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops

```
aa[j][i] = (aa[j][i-1] + aa[j-1][i]) / 1.9
```
Important: `aa[j][i-1]` is the **newly computed** value for the same row `j` when `i-1 < i` in the current iteration of the `i` loop, but `aa[j-1][i]` is from the **previous row** from the **previous iteration of `j` loop** (or from initial data if `j=1`).  
This is a **wavefront** pattern: dependence on left (`i-1`) and top (`j-1`).  

**Optimized code logic:**  
It attempts to compute row by row using:  
- `prev_row` = previous row’s final values (from last `j` iteration)  
- `left_val` = current row’s newly computed value for `i-1`  
- `temp = (left_val + prev_row[i]) / 1.9f`  

But there’s a critical bug:  
In source code, `aa[j][i-1]` is the **updated** value from the **current iteration of `i` loop**.  
In optimized code, `left_val` is indeed that updated value, so that part is correct.  

But `aa[j-1][i]` in source code is **not** necessarily `prev_row[i]` in optimized code.  
Because `prev_row[i]` is set to `aa[j-1][i]` **before** the `j` loop starts for row `j`.  
But in source code, `aa[j-1][i]` might have been updated earlier in the same `nl` iteration when `j-1` was processed.  
Wait — careful:  
When we are at `j=5`, `i=10`, `aa[4][10]` was computed in the **previous iteration of `j`** (when `j=4`). That’s exactly `prev_row[10]` if we set `prev_row` to `aa[4]` after finishing `j=4`.  
That seems correct.  

Let’s test with a small example:  

**Initial 3x3 array:**  
```
1 1 1
1 1 1
1 1 1
```

**Source code step for nl=1, j=1,i=1:**  
`aa[1][1] = (aa[1][0] + aa[0][1])/1.9 = (1 + 1)/1.9 = 1.05263`  

**Optimized code:**  
`prev_row` = row 0 = `[1,1,1]`  
`current_row` = row 1 = `[1,1,1]`  
`left_val = current_row[0] = 1`  
i=1: `temp = (1 + prev_row[1])/1.9 = (1+1)/1.9 = 1.05263` ✅  
So far matches.  

Next i=2:  
Source: `aa[1][2] = (aa[1][1] + aa[0][2])/1.9 = (1.05263 + 1)/1.9 = 1.08033`  
Optimized: `left_val = 1.05263`, `prev_row[2]=1`, `temp = (1.05263+1)/1.9 = 1.08033` ✅  

So row 1 matches.  

Now j=2,i=1:  
Source: `aa[2][1] = (aa[2][0] + aa[1][1])/1.9 = (1 + 1.05263)/1.9 = 1.08033`  
Optimized: `prev_row` = row 1 final = `[1, 1.05263, 1.08033]`  
`current_row` = row 2 initial = `[1,1,1]`  
`left_val = 1`  
i=1: `temp = (1 + prev_row[1])/1.9 = (1 + 1.05263)/1.9 = 1.08033` ✅  

i=2:  
Source: `aa[2][2] = (aa[2][1] + aa[1][2])/1.9 = (1.08033 + 1.08033)/1.9 = 1.13719`  
Optimized: `left_val=1.08033`, `prev_row[2]=1.08033`, `temp=(1.08033+1.08033)/1.9=1.13719` ✅  

So semantics match.

## **3. Vectorization analysis**  

Compiler says:  
> remark: loop not vectorized: value that could not be identified as reduction is used outside the loop  

This refers to the inner `i` loop:  
```c
float left_val = current_row[0];
for (int i = 1; i < 256; i++) {
    float temp = (left_val + prev_row[i]) / 1.9f;
    aa[j][i] = temp;
    left_val = temp;   // loop-carried dependency
}
```
`left_val` is carried from iteration `i` to `i+1`. This is a **true dependency** (serial computation along the row).  
Automatic vectorization fails because each iteration depends on the previous iteration’s computed `left_val`.  

This is inherent to the recurrence in the source code (wavefront along row).  
But note: the source code’s inner loop also has this same recurrence (`aa[j][i-1]` is updated just before used in next `i`).  

So the optimized code didn’t improve vectorization; it just made the recurrence explicit in a scalar variable `left_val`.

## **4. Optimization opportunity**  

The recurrence along `i` prevents straightforward vectorization of the inner loop.  
But the loop over `j` is parallelizable after a skewing transformation (wavefront parallel).  
However, the given optimized code does **not** enable vectorization; it still has the same scalar dependency.  

We could try **outer loop vectorization** (vectorize over `j` for fixed `i` with skew), but that’s not done here.  
So the optimized code is **not fully optimized** for vectorization.  

Given the compiler feedback, the code is **not** vectorized, so there is optimization opportunity → **FAIL**.

Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1

   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];
     ```  

   ```c
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments

**Application Rules**:

   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```

    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:

     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body

     ```c
     // Original

     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops

- Flow dependence on `aa[j][i-1]` (same row, previous column, updated earlier in this `j` iteration).  
- Flow dependence on `aa[j-1][i]` (previous row, same column, updated in previous `j` iteration).


## **2. Semantic comparison**

The original code computes:

```c
aa[j][i] = (aa[j][i-1] + aa[j-1][i]) / 1.9;
```

The optimized code attempts to avoid dependencies by storing `aa[j][i-1]` in `left_val` and `aa[j-1][i]` in `prev_row[i]`.

However, there’s a critical bug:  
In the original, `aa[j][i-1]` is the **newly computed** value from the same row `j` at column `i-1` (after it was updated earlier in the same `i` loop).  
The optimized code correctly tracks this by `left_val = new_val` after computing `new_val`.

But `prev_row[i]` is supposed to be `aa[j-1][i]` **before** any updates in the current `j` iteration.  
The optimized code loads `prev_row[i]` from `aa[0][i]` at the start of each `j` loop, but after `j=1`, it updates `prev_row` from `aa[j][i]` **after** computing row `j`. That’s correct for the next iteration of `j`.

Let’s test with a small example.

---

**Example 1:**  
Initial `aa` all zeros except `aa[0][1] = 1.9`, `aa[1][0] = 1.9`.

Original, `j=1, i=1`:  
`aa[1][1] = (aa[1][0] + aa[0][1]) / 1.9 = (1.9 + 1.9) / 1.9 = 2.0`.

Optimized:  
`prev_row[1] = aa[0][1] = 1.9`  
`left_val = current_row[0] = aa[1][0] = 1.9`  
`new_val = (1.9 + 1.9) / 1.9 = 2.0` ✅ matches.

---

**Example 2:**  
Now test dependency on newly computed `aa[j][i-1]`.

Initial: `aa[1][0] = 1.9`, `aa[0][1] = 0`, `aa[0][2] = 0`.

Original, `j=1, i=1`: `aa[1][1] = (1.9 + 0) / 1.9 = 1.0`.  
`i=2`: `aa[1][2] = (aa[1][1] + aa[0][2]) / 1.9 = (1.0 + 0) / 1.9 ≈ 0.5263`.

Optimized:  
`i=1`: `new_val = (1.9 + 0) / 1.9 = 1.0`, `left_val = 1.0`.  
`i=2`: `new_val = (1.0 + 0) / 1.9 ≈ 0.5263` ✅ matches.

---

**Example 3:**  
Check `j=2` after `j=1` computed.

Initial:  
Row 0: all 0 except `aa[0][1] = 1.9`.  
Row 1: after computation in Example 2: `aa[1][0]=1.9`, `aa[1][1]=1.0`, `aa[1][2]≈0.5263`.

Original `j=2, i=1`:  
`aa[2][1] = (aa[2][0] + aa[1][1]) / 1.9`.  
Assume `aa[2][0]` initial = 0, `aa[1][1] = 1.0` → `(0 + 1.0)/1.9 ≈ 0.5263`.

Optimized:  
At start of `j=2`, `prev_row[i]` was set from `aa[1][i]` at end of `j=1` loop.  
So `prev_row[1] = 1.0`.  
`current_row[0] = aa[2][0] = 0`.  
`i=1`: `new_val = (0 + 1.0) / 1.9 ≈ 0.5263` ✅ matches.

So **semantics are correct** in the optimized version.

## **3. Vectorization analysis**

The compiler says:  
> loop not vectorized: value that could not be identified as reduction is used outside the loop

This refers to the inner `i` loop in optimized code:

```c
for (int i = 1; i < 256; i++) {
    float new_val = (left_val + prev_row[i]) / 1.9f;
    aa[j][i] = new_val;
    left_val = new_val;
}
```

`left_val` is a loop-carried scalar dependency (each iteration uses `left_val` from previous iteration), which is not a reduction (reduction would be like sum += something). This is a recurrence (serial dependency), so automatic vectorization fails.

But note: the original loop also has the same recurrence (`aa[j][i-1]` depends on previous iteration in `i`), so it’s inherently not vectorizable in the `i` loop without changing algorithm.

However, the optimized code’s structure doesn’t improve vectorizability — it still has the same recurrence.  
The compiler remark is just telling us it can’t vectorize that loop.

---

## **4. Optimization opportunity**

The optimized code introduces extra copying (`current_row`, `prev_row`) which may hurt performance more than help.  
It doesn’t enable vectorization; it just rearranges the same dependency.

To make vectorization possible, we’d need to change the algorithm (e.g., use wavefront parallelization along diagonals), but that changes semantics unless carefully done.

Given the recurrence `aa[j][i] = f(aa[j][i-1], aa[j-1][i])`, vectorization in `i` is impossible directly.  
But vectorization in `j` is possible for fixed `i` if we reorder loops (but that changes dependency pattern).

Since the optimized code doesn’t improve vectorization and adds overhead, it’s **not fully optimized** — there’s an opportunity to improve by removing unnecessary copies or considering alternative transformations.

---

**Conclusion:**  
Semantics are correct, but vectorization fails due to recurrence, and the current “optimized” code has extra memory operations without enabling vectorization → **FAIL** (optimization opportunity exists).

The optimized code loads `prev_row[i]` from `aa[0][i]` at the start of each `j` loop, but after `j=1`, it updates `prev_row` from `aa[j][i]` **after** computing row `j`. That’s correct for the next iteration of `j`.

Let’s test with a small example.

---

**Example 1:**  
Initial `aa` all zeros except `aa[0][1] = 1.9`, `aa[1][0] = 1.9`.

Original, `j=1, i=1`:  
`aa[1][1] = (aa[1][0] + aa[0][1]) / 1.9 = (1.9 + 1.9) / 1.9 = 2.0`.

Optimized:  
`prev_row[1] = aa[0][1] = 1.9`  
`left_val = current_row[0] = aa[1][0] = 1.9`  
`new_val = (1.9 + 1.9) / 1.9 = 2.0` ✅ matches.

---

**Example 2:**  
Now test dependency on newly computed `aa[j][i-1]`.

Initial: `aa[1][0] = 1.9`, `aa[0][1] = 0`, `aa[0][2] = 0`.

Original, `j=1, i=1`: `aa[1][1] = (1.9 + 0) / 1.9 = 1.0`.  
`i=2`: `aa[1][2] = (aa[1][1] + aa[0][2]) / 1.9 = (1.0 + 0) / 1.9 ≈ 0.5263`.

Optimized:  
`i=1`: `new_val = (1.9 + 0) / 1.9 = 1.0`, `left_val = 1.0`.  
`i=2`: `new_val = (1.0 + 0) / 1.9 ≈ 0.5263` ✅ matches.

---

**Example 3:**  
Check `j=2` after `j=1` computed.

Initial:  
Row 0: all 0 except `aa[0][1] = 1.9`.  
Row 1: after computation in Example 2: `aa[1][0]=1.9`, `aa[1][1]=1.0`, `aa[1][2]≈0.5263`.

Original `j=2, i=1`:  
`aa[2][1] = (aa[2][0] + aa[1][1]) / 1.9`.  
Assume `aa[2][0]` initial = 0, `aa[1][1] = 1.0` → `(0 + 1.0)/1.9 ≈ 0.5263`.

Optimized:  
At start of `j=2`, `prev_row[i]` was set from `aa[1][i]` at end of `j=1` loop.  
So `prev_row[1] = 1.0`.  
`current_row[0] = aa[2][0] = 0`.  
`i=1`: `new_val = (0 + 1.0) / 1.9 ≈ 0.5263` ✅ matches.

So **semantics are correct** in the optimized version.

---

## **3. Vectorization analysis**

The compiler says:  
> loop not vectorized: value that could not be identified as reduction is used outside the loop

This refers to the inner `i` loop in optimized code:

```c
for (int i = 1; i < 256; i++) {
    float new_val = (left_val + prev_row[i]) / 1.9f;
    aa[j][i] = new_val;
    left_val = new_val;
}
```

`left_val` is a loop-carried scalar dependency (each iteration uses `left_val` from previous iteration), which is not a reduction (reduction would be like sum += something). This is a recurrence (serial dependency), so automatic vectorization fails.

But note: the original loop also has the same recurrence (`aa[j][i-1]` depends on previous iteration in `i`), so it’s inherently not vectorizable in the `i` loop without changing algorithm.

However, the optimized code’s structure doesn’t improve vectorizability — it still has the same recurrence.  
The compiler remark is just telling us it can’t vectorize that loop.

---

## **4. Optimization opportunity**

The optimized code introduces extra copying (`current_row`, `prev_row`) which may hurt performance more than help.  
It doesn’t enable vectorization; it just rearranges the same dependency.

To make vectorization possible, we’d need to change the algorithm (e.g., use wavefront parallelization along diagonals), but that changes semantics unless carefully done.

Given the recurrence `aa[j][i] = f(aa[j][i-1], aa[j-1][i])`, vectorization in `i` is impossible directly.  
But vectorization in `j` is possible for fixed `i` if we reorder loops (but that changes dependency pattern).

Since the optimized code doesn’t improve vectorization and adds overhead, it’s **not fully optimized** — there’s an opportunity to improve by removing unnecessary copies or considering alternative transformations.

---

**Conclusion:**  
Semantics are correct, but vectorization fails due to recurrence, and the current “optimized” code has extra memory operations without enabling vectorization → **FAIL** (optimization opportunity exists).

---

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];
     ```  

   ```c
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];
   ```  

   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses

```c
for (int nl = 0; nl < 100*(iterations/(256)); nl++) {
```

```c
            aa[j][i] = (aa[j][i-1] + aa[j-1][i]) / 1.9;
        }

```
This is a **wavefront** (or stencil) computation:  
- `aa[j][i]` depends on `aa[j][i-1]` (updated earlier in same row in this iteration) and `aa[j-1][i]` (from previous row, already updated in this `nl` iteration).  
- This means within each `nl` iteration, the updates propagate diagonally across the array.

```c
    float left_val = aa[j][0];
    for (int i = 1; i < 256; i++) {
        float new_val = (left_val + prev_row[i]) / 1.9f;
        aa[j][i] = new_val;
        left_val = new_val;
    }
    for (int i = 0; i < 256; i++) {
        prev_row[i] = aa[j][i];
    }
}
```

`left_val` stores the **current nl iteration’s** value from `aa[j][i-1]`.  
This matches the original dependencies **within the same nl iteration** because `aa[j-1][i]` is taken from `prev_row[i]`, which was updated in the previous row’s inner loop in this same `nl` iteration.

Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```

    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:

     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:

   - No interleaved access between distributed loops

   - Example pattern:

     ```c
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2
     ```  

   *Implementation*:

   ```c
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];

   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];
   ```  

     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     ```  

## **2. Semantic comparison with simple test cases**

The original loop:

```c
for (int j = 1; j < 256; j++) {

```

Dependencies:  
- `aa[j][i-1]` is the newly computed value from the same row in the **current iteration of i-1** (within same j-loop iteration).  
- `aa[j-1][i]` is from the previous row, computed in the **previous j-loop iteration**.

This is a **wavefront** pattern: each cell depends on left neighbor (same row, current iteration) and top neighbor (previous row).

---

The optimized code:

```c
float prev_row[256]; // initially row 0
for (int j = 1; j < 256; j++) {
    float left_val = aa[j][0];
    for (int i = 1; i < 256; i++) {
        float new_val = (left_val + prev_row[i]) / 1.9f;
        aa[j][i] = new_val;
        left_val = new_val;
    }
    // update prev_row for next j
    for (int i = 0; i < 256; i++) {
        prev_row[i] = aa[j][i];
    }
}
```

```
4 5 6
7 8 9
```

`new_val = (4 + 2)/1.9 = 3.15789` ✅ same.

Now check j=1, i=2 in original:  
`aa[1][2] = (aa[1][1] + aa[0][2]) / 1.9`  
`aa[1][1]` is already updated to 3.15789, `aa[0][2]` = 3  
→ `(3.15789 + 3)/1.9 ≈ 3.241`

Optimized:  
`left_val` = 3.15789, `prev_row[2]` = 3  
→ `(3.15789 + 3)/1.9 ≈ 3.241` ✅ same.

So for j=1, it matches.

Now j=2, i=1 in original:  
`aa[2][1] = (aa[2][0] + aa[1][1]) / 1.9`  
`aa[2][0]` = 7, `aa[1][1]` = 3.15789

Optimized:

`left_val` = `aa[2][0]` = 7  
→ `(7 + 3.15789)/1.9 ≈ 5.34626` ✅ same.

Thus **semantics are preserved** in the optimized code.

## **3. Vectorization analysis**

The compiler remark says:  
> loop not vectorized: value that could not be identified as reduction is used outside the loop

This refers to the inner loop over `i`:

```c
for (int i = 1; i < 256; i++) {
    float new_val = (left_val + prev_row[i]) / 1.9f;
    aa[j][i] = new_val;
    left_val = new_val;
}
```

Here `left_val` is carried across iterations (serial dependency), so it’s not a reduction but a **recurrence** (1st-order).  
This prevents vectorization because each iteration depends on the previous iteration’s `left_val`.

However, note that `prev_row[i]` is read-only in this loop, so the recurrence is only on `left_val`, not on memory.  
This is essentially a **scan** (prefix) pattern:  
`left_val[i] = (left_val[i-1] + prev_row[i]) / 1.9`.

Such a recurrence is not parallelizable in a straightforward way for floating point unless using specialized parallel prefix algorithms, which the compiler cannot auto-vectorize.

---

## **4. Can we improve vectorization?**

The original loop had two dependencies:  
1. Horizontal: `aa[j][i-1]` (same as `left_val` recurrence)  
2. Vertical: `aa[j-1][i]` (solved by `prev_row`)

The horizontal recurrence is the blocker.  
To vectorize, we could try **loop skewing** or **wavefront parallelization**, but that changes iteration order and may affect floating-point rounding.

Alternatively, we could compute in chunks using **strip-mining** but keep the recurrence inside each chunk serial.

However, the optimized code already improves cache locality (single `prev_row` cache line reuse), but the horizontal recurrence remains.

Given the compiler feedback, **automatic vectorization is not possible** due to the recurrence.

---

## **5. Optimization opportunity**

Yes, there is an opportunity:  
We could try **outer loop vectorization** over `i` by using **temporal blocking** or **recomputing** parts, but that’s complex and may not match semantics exactly due to floating point.

But the question says: if vectorization is not possible, suggest potential changes.  
One change: use **SIMD for the vertical part** by computing multiple rows at once (j-loop unrolling) but horizontal recurrence still serial per row.

Given the constraints, the optimized code is already good for memory but not auto-vectorizable due to recurrence.  
We could try **loop interchange** but that’s invalid due to dependencies.

Thus, **further optimization** is possible in theory (manual SIMD using parallel prefix), but not automatically.

---

**Final decision:**  
Semantics are correct, but vectorization fails due to recurrence, and there is still optimization opportunity (manual vectorization with parallel scan).

Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c

       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  

   *Implementation*:

   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1
   ```  

   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];
     ```  

   ```c
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments

**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c

     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  

    ```c
     b[i] = y * z;

     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses


This is a **wavefront** pattern:  
- `aa[j][i-1]` is from the same row, computed earlier in this same `j` iteration.  
- `aa[j-1][i]` is from the previous row, computed in the previous `j` iteration of the same `nl` loop.  

The computation order within each `nl` is important:  
When computing `aa[j][i]`, `aa[j][i-1]` is the *new* value from earlier in this row, not the old value from before `nl` started.

---

### Optimized algorithm:

In the original, when computing `aa[j][i]`, `aa[j-1][i]` is the value from **the current `nl` iteration’s previous row**, which may have been updated earlier in this same `nl` iteration (for `j-1`).  
In the optimized version, `prev_row[i]` is loaded at the start of the `j` loop as `aa[j-1][i]` **before** the inner `i` loop begins.  
But `aa[j-1][i]` for `i > 1` depends on `aa[j-1][i-1]` which was updated in the *previous* `j` iteration’s inner loop — that’s fine because `prev_row` captured it after the previous `j` iteration finished.

**Objective**: Generate a semantically equivalent vectorize-friendly version of `*_opt` using:  
**Preservation Requirements**:  
1. Function signature

If the original value is used, e.g. a[i+1] is used for iteration i, a[i+1] should be stored in a temporary variable(use memcpy to store orign value) or used before updating a[i+1]
If the updated value is used, e.g. a[i-1] is used for iteration i, a[i-1] should update before using it.

**Possible Transformations**  
Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops  

Use temporary variable:
Use temp variable to store result to avoid complex memory access

Replace multi-way conditionals with arithmetic masking, ensuring uniform execution paths across iterations.

     ```c
    // Original
    if (cond1)  
        x = value_A;  
    else if (cond2)  
        x = value_B;  
    else  
        x = value_C;
    // Optimized  
    x = cond1 * value_A + (cond2 && !cond1) * value_B + (!cond1 && !cond2) * value_C;  
     ```

**Objective**: Generate a semantically equivalent vectorize-friendly version of `*_opt` using:  
**Preservation Requirements**:  
1. Function signature

If the original value is used, e.g. a[i+1] is used for iteration i, a[i+1] should be stored in a temporary variable(use memcpy to store orign value) or used before updating a[i+1]
If the updated value is used, e.g. a[i-1] is used for iteration i, a[i-1] should update before using it.

**Possible Transformations**  
Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops  

Use temporary variable:
Use temp variable to store result to avoid complex memory access

Remove branching logic (if, else if, switch) from loops to enable automatic vectorization:
Replace multi-way conditionals with arithmetic masking, ensuring uniform execution paths across iterations.
**When to Apply**:  
    - Example:  
     ```c
    // Original
    if (cond1)  
        x = value_A;  
    else if (cond2)  
        x = value_B;  
    else  
        x = value_C;
    // Optimized  
    x = cond1 * value_A + (cond2 && !cond1) * value_B + (!cond1 && !cond2) * value_C;  
     ```

    int loop_count = 100 * (iterations / 256);

            }
        }

}

**Objective**: Generate a semantically equivalent vectorize-friendly version of `*_opt` using:  
**Preservation Requirements**:  
1. Function signature

If the original value is used, e.g. a[i+1] is used for iteration i, a[i+1] should be stored in a temporary variable(use memcpy to store orign value) or used before updating a[i+1]
If the updated value is used, e.g. a[i-1] is used for iteration i, a[i-1] should update before using it.

**Possible Transformations**  
Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops  

Use temporary variable:
Use temp variable to store result to avoid complex memory access

Remove branching logic (if, else if, switch) from loops to enable automatic vectorization:
Replace multi-way conditionals with arithmetic masking, ensuring uniform execution paths across iterations.
**When to Apply**:  
    - Example:  
     ```c
    // Original
    if (cond1)  
        x = value_A;  
    else if (cond2)  
        x = value_B;  
    else  
        x = value_C;
    // Optimized  
    x = cond1 * value_A + (cond2 && !cond1) * value_B + (!cond1 && !cond2) * value_C;  
     ```

Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed

     ```

   - No interleaved access between distributed loops

Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body

     ```c
     // Original

       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
     ```  

## **2. Semantic comparison**  

The original code:  

```c
aa[j][i] = (aa[j][i-1] + aa[j-1][i]) / 1.9;
```

- `aa[j][i-1]` is the element to the left in the **same row** `j`, already updated in the **current iteration** of `i` loop.
- `aa[j-1][i]` is the element directly above in the **previous row** `j-1`, which was updated in the **previous iteration** of `j` loop.

This is a **wavefront** (or stencil) dependency:  
Each cell depends on its **left neighbor** (same row, same iteration of `j`) and **top neighbor** (previous row, previous iteration of `j`).

---

The optimized code uses:  

```c
float top_val = prev_row[i];
float left_val = temp_row[i-1];
temp_row[i] = (left_val + top_val) / 1.9f;
```

Here `prev_row[i]` holds `aa[j-1][i]` from the previous row.  
`temp_row[i-1]` holds the newly computed value for `aa[j][i-1]` in this row.  
This matches the dependency pattern exactly.

But there’s a subtlety:  
In the original code, `aa[j][i-1]` is read from the **original array** after it was updated earlier in the same `j` loop iteration.  
In the optimized code, `temp_row[i-1]` is the newly computed value, not read from `aa[j][i-1]` in memory. That’s fine — it’s the same value.

However, the **initialization** of `temp_row[0] = aa[j][0];` is correct because `aa[j][0]` is never updated in the original loop (since `i` starts at 1).  
And `prev_row[i]` is initialized from `aa[0][i]` at the start of each `j` loop.

Optimized code, `j=1`:  
`prev_row = [1,1,1]`  
`temp_row[0] = aa[1][0] = 2`  
`i=1`: `top_val=1`, `left_val=2` → `temp_row[1] = 3/1.9 ≈ 1.5789` ✅  

`i=2`: `top_val=1`, `left_val=1.5789` → `temp_row[2] = (1.5789+1)/1.9 ≈ 1.3573`  

Original code, `j=1, i=2`:  
`aa[1][2] = (aa[1][1] + aa[0][2])/1.9 = (1.5789 + 1)/1.9 ≈ 1.3573` ✅  

So the optimized code matches the original semantics.
